  These mean exactly the same thing, here's why:
  "I'm coming with you" is like, I'm going to stay beside you as we go 
  "I'm going with you" is like, I'm going to travel over there with you
  
  I can't think of any situation where you would want to use one instead of the other, and I think I use both equally.

**@carinazh** (👍0 · 2013-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lqtFCGkSTM4&lc=UgxMOSZyKMcJ9NQDw0Z4AaABAg

**@rickygervais1776** (👍0 · 2012-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lqtFCGkSTM4&lc=UgyjKdxZaq5iXDN5GdN4AaABAg
  'Come back' means to return to where you are right now (in the present). Go back means to return to where you were (in the past). "Come back and visit us" is what you would say to someone who was "going back" to his home country. You could also say "I will come back to America after I go back to Germany" for example. This means that you are in America now and will return to America after you go home to Germany. Hope that helps.

  After you "made" the homework you have a physical thing, the paper with your writing on it. You made that. Now the way it is used in English puts more emphasis on the act of doing it. In German the phrase would be "Ich (I) habe meine (have my) Hausaufgaben (homework) gemacht (made)" 
  The explanation about do and make is illogical. It is a passabel mnemonic probably, but I would say it's still arbitrary. Mostly it reminds me of how teachers like to be wise-asses.

      Usually when you add the "do" it is emphasizing how much you love the person  "Gosh, I really do you love you." Or you say it when answering a question. "Do you love me?" "Yes, I do love you."   Both " a few " and "few " are used for countable nouns , but " few " has negative meaning. Check out the e.g
  . I have few friends ( = not many friends , not enough )
   I have a few friends ( = some friends , enough )
  Little/A little is used for uncountable noun and used in the same way as " few/ a few"
  Hopefully it's useful :)

  Your videos on How to Write an Effective Essay were awesome and I love the way you taught them! This video was good too but for me there could have been a bit more explanation to why we would do these steps, in mastering our vocabulary. For example a big part that made me want to make this response is that when you told me how to spell a word and remember it. Write it out ten times right? I know this is an effective way of remembering how to spell a word, but only because of experience (trial and error). If you could explain to your viewers why this is beneficial and what evidence has shown that might lead to this connection of memory (in a simple and understanding way) it would be A LOT more appealing to your viewers!! And purely from experience, i was told this when i was in grade 3-4-5 and just though of it as more work. Of course I may have been a bit young to really grasp the concept of why it helps, but if it could have been explained to me as to why it worked and told to me again and again, it would have stuck with me up till now and would have helped a lot through my high school years. I am now in grade 12 and I love to learn! A huge influence for me has been psychology! Learning about the brain and why we think the way we do and why everything works! By leaning all this it brought me to finding out how memory works! It's intriguing! What i'm really trying to get across (from a students point of view) is that there is a massive limit on what a student can achieve unless he/she fully understands (in their own though process) as to why something works. Like telling a student a bunch of formulas in math to work out vertical and horizontal velocity. At first I had just a bunch of numbers and letters thrown at me until my teacher had guided me through explaining why each one worked out and effected each other. Your style of teaching is brilliant!! Just maybe keep in mind to explain as to why doing certain steps work so well in learning what ever you do.! :D

  @3:22
  How to learn the word 'definition'?
  1st: You need to understand it, you need to look up the definition of the word 'definition' in the dictionary.
  2nd: You pronounce it.
  3rd: YOU SPELL IT, D-E-F-I-N-I-T-I-O-N. Good? Now do it backwards....
  4th: You recognize it, 'recognize' that it's spelled D-E-F-I-N-I-T-I-O-N. The trick is to 'recognize' that there's a letter "I" between the 2 letters N and T

**@OrcjaEnglish** (👍0 · 2025-09-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FI58xq9oP7g&lc=Ugzt2sRFqYPBfBdGPrJ4AaABAg
  Are you struggling to improve your vocabulary? Do you often feel embarrassed while having conversations with your colleagues due to your poor choice of words? Today, I will share a strategy proposed by Sir James, a famous English YouTuber with 4.77 million subscribers. There are four sections of this method: first, understand; second, pronounce; third, spell; fourth, recognize. Let's talk a little bit more about each component.
  
  First, understand: You should look for new words in the dictionary. Then, find multiple definitions of each word and use that word in three to four sentences.
  
  Second, pronounce: You should learn how to pronounce it correctly.
  
  Third, spell: You have to memorize its correct spelling.
  
  Fourth, recognize: You should recognize when someone is speaking and pronouncing that word.
  
  Following this method, you can effectively master your vocabulary. Thank you.

**@AfreensHair** (👍6 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fm2m4CHEWnc&lc=UgjK4RhQkNvHKngCoAEC
  James, I just watched your 'must & have to' video from November 2008 (which reminded me of shall vs will, but that's another topic). In any case I was hoping you could address a peculiarity I have noticed with some English language learners when expressing the negative:  They seem to think that because "must" and "have to" can be used interchangeably THEN "must not" and "do not have to" are also synonyms, which is NOT the case. To illustrate:  they would say "You children don't have to play in the street" - which implies some kind of benign choice, whereas "You children mustn't play in the street" is the compulsory command they are trying to express. Can you PLEASE address this, because I have noticed it is confusing for some people. Thank you!

  I used to teach English Language in the Faculty of Economics at my local university and I remember a story told me by my chief. Only when I became a teacher did I start to understand all the requirements spoken about in this video and teach students how to write a good essay. With regard to evoking the reader's interest we told them to aim to give the reader an 'ahaa!' experience, ie, when reading the essay the reader should say 'Ahaa! Here is someone who understands what it's about!'

  I remember approaching a professor of law during my American law school days to ask how an exam essay I wrote warranted the grade I received and how I might improve it. This response struck me as lazy and divorced from any interest in my improvement as a law student.  And this experience yielded my determination to never financially contribute to this school even after a long career in law that I might attribute to my legal education.  By contrast, Professor Williams' video provides the kind of formulaic instruction that I'd hope all academics would feel it important to share with their students.   Bravo Professor Williams ! !

  When wandering around a bookshop about 30 years ago I came across a book about how a scientist could adapt their writing style to grab and be interesting to the non specialist. I tried it, and it worked. I found myself writing for THES, general newspapers,  motorcycle magazines, and other diverse organs. It would have been useful to have been introduced to this writing approach as an undergrad to prepare us for popularising our subjects.

  Small warnings to danes or English speakers in denmark. Don't formulate yourself the way you would in English, don't be different and don't be recognisable. The danish system allows for more freedom in the department of failing you because they don't like you or your writing style. This is both for language based essays but is also applicable to Chemistry, Biology, Physics, and Mathematics. 
  To this day I still remember a teacher of mine telling me he gave me a lower mark on a specific project because he didn't "gel" with my writing style... in Chemistry. Of course this had nothing to do with him constantly questioning where I was "really" from and regularly pointing out that one of my names is clearly foreign (apparently he couldn't tell the difference between Italian and Greek, which I still find funny any time I think of him).

**@ceh5526** (👍1 · 2025-10-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jztZpqnDw8A&lc=UgzBlutBlrrMBCEBt4J4AaABAg
  I've noticed that essays in the USA expect a 'literature review' pretty early on, and it seems to have a significant weight for marking. In the UK this review would more likely be in the penultimate section of the essay, so that the student shows awareness of often varying and competing scholarly opinions. After this the concluding section would be the student's assessment and thought on the subject. 
  This difference can also be found, I think, between taught components of an America doctoral program (sic), and the research doctorate in the UK. The students think differently, and have different-is expectations of supervisions, etc. This has been my experience.

  Theory, great option, because there are so many variations on the theme, far more than Elgar wrote. Stumble on to the significant facts and you lose your reader. Like the clever game of hampton court maze, go the right way and youll be alone. So, Chamberlain presents his variations on a theme while noting the significant facts to himself, and all are satisfied, he pursued appeasement and it failed. Theory, theory, theory, but Chamberlain of course knew the significant facts. Essayists like to claim that Hitlers aims were significant facts, but they werent, the significant facts were what would, using the MI5 terminology of 1938, quietly cramp Hitlers style, because there were obvious dangers in him having power, Chamberlains view in 1932, and he evidently refused to change his mind. So, keep to theories and avoid facts for top marks, facts just dont cut any cookies, but remember what the top marks say about you, youre probably still wandering in the maze scratching your head.

  I think one of the advantages that students from expensive private schools have is that, prior to university, they will already been taught age 14-18 by chaps exactly like the academic in this video. They will have been carefully instructed, perhaps even in one-to-one tutorials, how to structure essays, how to define terms, how to think critically, how to marshal facts, how to use citations, how to argue, etc. Of course, if an individual is weak academically then no amount of tutoring and coaching is going to make him or her into a star student. But I'm pretty certain there is a not-insignificant section of the population who are perhaps just a little above average academically; and for these people the school may make all the difference. I don't have a strong view whether this apparent unfairness is something that should be (or could be) rectified. It would be naive not to suppose that the children of wealthy people are always going to have an advantage in all areas of life. It is what it is, I guess...

**@make_lemonade21** (👍1 · 2025-12-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jztZpqnDw8A&lc=Ugxx-bTfytOp26YUyIh4AaABAg
  Interestingly, other cultures might have a different idea of how a good essay should be structured, as described by Kaplan (1966). For example, the Romance/German style allows for digressions (instead of just getting straight to the point), Asians are usually quite indirect in their writing, and so on. Also, we need to consider if the language is hearer or speaker based. English is speaker-based so it's the writer's responsibility to make sure the reader understands the text. In hearer-based cultures, however, this might be seen as "overexplaining" or being treated as a child and is, therefore, impolite.
  Also, there are smaller things, such using 'we' instead of 'I'. For example, in traditional Russian academic writing, even if you're a single author, using 'I' in a paper is frowned upon (although it's slowly becoming a thing of the past). In English, however, it is forbidden, even (at least according to APA)

We are open to a wide range of claims, provided they are grounded in good theory and evidence. Yes, spelling and grammar lapses can lead to lower marks, but reasonable adjustments (such as extra time) are made for those that might struggle with such things. Colloquialisms are not ideal, because essay writing is quite formal. Idioms can be acceptable, but might signal a lack of thought.

  You are - if you're alluding Derrida - using the word 'deconstruct' incorrectly. 
  
  It is not, in Derrida's philosophy, a verb. A text is always already in a state of deconstruction. Say 'analyse' or 'disassemble' instead if you want to be accurate.
  
  Having studied Deconstruction for many years, I can tell you it is a mark of people who did not check the etymology of the word (if alluding to Derrida), which is ironic, really, given Derrida's actual ideas about what derails texts and logos.

  They're happily giving good marks to chatgpt. Module results have to be kept up but we tell the story to students about essay meaning to try and test ideas etc... i've done this shtick and spiel myself
  
  50s used to be good description. But he says pretty much inaccessible, poor.  60s used to be good synthesis, i.e. critical contrasts to draw implications. 
  
  I"d worry about suggesting knockabout boris journalistic interest style as well as many 'layers' for topmost marks.. there's limited space in essays. Let the evidence speak... that's a skill in itself. Efficiency.. making sure each paragraph is a machine... has a key issue to address, and end of para has a direct implication for the question before moving on. Thread is then less important, we tell studes to make sure each para has its own purpose, this can help cover the range he's talking about.. method issues etc and diversifies coverage. Intro is v important for us.. background is waste of space where marks could be had instead, so we work on setting out a rationale at the start. Often overlooked,  even toughest part, but good intro rationale can radically help writing... and use of space.
  Bunch of implications in essay to draw together at the end ... conclusion from that can also draw wider implications for the science, we give marks for that, it's in the highest level in rubric. Bit more than focus focus, drawing consequences for the approach etc.  Original and excellent are vague, if those are in your rubric perhaps challenge. Btw he hasn't mentioned here it's different year on year. By final undergrad year question should be pitched at 'critically evaluate' anyway and answer therefore should have some of that to pass. Whereas first yr might ask to discuss and the critical evaluation would then just be high mark. Rubric should shift a step each year so there's some kind of development upward
  
  Or perhaps they don't distinguish there.  Framework for higher education has benchmarks for level 4 5 6 7.  Masters for us is critical eval for pass, difference is emphasis on making judgments about practice, making practical recommendations.  Defendable beyond just saying more evidence or knockabout argument.

  When he argues that the best essays are the ones that "are most interesting", what he is really saying is that the essays that score highest are the ones the professors like the most. Because fundamentally this is a subjective determination. What is most interesting about this insight is that, in order to achieve a high grade and advance your 'career' as a student, you must try to determine what is it that professors at these institutions desire most. Subsequently, you must mold yourself into the academic culture to which you are held sway. I don't believe this would be too tragic if it were simply that students at higher education institutions were planning to become professors themselves as yes, they should seek to understand the culture of the academic community if they are to join it. But the purpose of attending higher education institutions is to make your way in the world. In other words, you have no (or at least few) good choices to attain success without entering this corrupted system. Fundamentally, academics represent a tiny minority of often highly insulated humans that feed of one another. The so-called 'ivory tower'. It is a terrible model and professors should be rotated out of their institutions and into the world of work. But they never will as they, as much as any other profession, are protective of their siloed existence.

  However, the problem that’s been facing academia and students arises from the unnecessary, arbitrary, misguided and misleading emphasis on Diversity, Equity and Inclusion, the insane notion that students have to be “protected” and therefore are not thinking critically, and the ideological politicisation of these institutions and the curricula.
  
  Unless students have had opportunity in their home setting or church to see past the indoctrination that has infected schools and a significant element of the teaching profession, they are not likely to enter university with an understanding of what it means critically to challenge the establishment’s assumptions. 
  
  Consequently they proceed through their studies with a dubious worldview unchecked, unchallenged and unchanged.
  
  That’s not a recipe for great, innovative, entrepreneurial or particularly intelligent contributors to the society for which we funded their education.
  
  Irrefutable evidence of how dangerous the current approach and system in universities is seen in the disgraceful, often hateful and violent suppression and cancellation by student bodies of visiting speakers whose viewpoints (or sometimes assumed viewpoints) they disagree with or dislike. Students of Jewish heritage have been particularly targeted, although the most of the antagonism appears to be levelled at fellow students, employees and visitors who hold Christian, traditional, socially and politically conservative values and views.
  
  An Act of Parliament has been necessary to remind universities of their statutory (let alone historic and moral) duty to encourage free speech and open debate.
  
  So, while it’s really good to see an explanation of effective essay writing, British universities also need a wholesale change to restore free speech and free thinking.

  To understand what an essay is,  read competent essayists — Read,  Seneca, Charles Lamb, Plato, Richard Steele, Thomas Carlyle, Oliver Goldsmith,  R. L. Stevenson,  Johnathan Swift, Mathew Arnold, F.R. Leavis,  Lionel Trilling, Jack London, G.B. Shaw, J.M. Keynes’ social commentary, Chaucer, the first three verses of The Prologue to the “Man of Lawe’s Tale”, Edmund Spenser’s ‘Fairie Queene”, Shakespeare.
  
  What is “ANALYSIS”? 
  
  How may we analyse “Aesop’s Fables” thoroughly?   Try asking yourself what single word describes the theme of one of the fables — In the “Hare and the Tortise” the theme is ‘Complacency’; in the “Fox and the Grapes”, the theme is “Cynicism” (“Sour Grapes’).    
  
  Suppose that you are to write an analysis of ‘The Hare and the Tortise”.    You may begin by noting that the fable exemplifies a rule for living well.  An analysis of any rule for living well would include a careful examination of the strengths and weaknesses of the proposed rule.
  
  So an analysis of that fable may begin by stating what the proposed rule for living is,  (explicitly); and then state in what way adherence to that rule may be not only helpful but possibly also unhelpful.  
  
  In the case of “The hare and the Tortise” the raw circumstances being used to illustrate a rule for living well — (i.e. not the rule itself) —  may be stated as, “The Hare lost the race because he did not try hard enough.”  
  
  Then as part of our analysis of the rule for living well we may put the contrary view as,    ‘Trying one’s hardest is not enough to attain one’s object’.   The rule for living well which is illustrated in the fable is ‘stay focused on your object’ — but it is possible to imagine that the principle of ‘remain focussed’ — if taken too narrowly — may also result in one’s failing to attain one’s object.  In the vernacular, we may say, that one may ‘try too hard’.    
   
  Then part of an analysis of the fable might include an illustration of exactly how that rule for living well may be over done.   In the circumstances of the fable — we may illustrate how an unduly narrow or over-zealous adherence to the proposed rule for living  may prove counter productive.    “If the Hare was trying too hard to simply go as fast as he possibly could,  he may not be paying sufficient attention to where he places his feet, and may catch his toe on a protruding tree root,  injure his limb and be rendered incapable of getting to the finish line ahead of the Tortise.” 
  
  Read British philosophers,  Hume, Berkeley, Hobbes, Locke, R.G. Collingwood, Bernard Bosanquet, A.N. Whitehead’s non-academic writing, (i.e. his social commentary and his published correspondence). (Whitehead’s academic writing is motivated by the very noblest motives, but it is impenetrable)
  
  A very useful example is F.H. Bradley’s, Essay III, in his “Ethical studies”, (page 85?)

      I don't mean to be dismissive, but there are several incorrect usages in your comment. 
      1. It's written as spontaneously, not spontaniously.
      2. Spontaneously sounds awkward with "Today, I have decided that I want", it's kind of like juggling a word that's higher level of English with the rest of sentence that's lower level. 
      3. There's an extra space after "I am". 
      4. "My just adequate" is incorrect grammar. It's supposed to be "functional English" or another word of your choice. 
      5. There's supposed to be a comma after "that reason" since there'd be a slight pause when you're reading the sentence irl. 
      6. 'Meticulously' sounds awkward with the rest of the sentence, just as before. It's kind of like C1 level word blended in a B1 level sentence. 
      6. 'I' is supposed to be capital when you're referring to yourself.
      7. 'Coherently' in this context is grammatically correct, but again, it's awkward. The better fit would be "fluently" in this context.
      
      Feel free to point out my inconsistencies too, especially the ones about the quotes.

**@hadar.shemesh [channel author]** (👍1 · 2026-07-30) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_6JxvW0FMQ8&lc=UgxJ548nbPxlBXgGM2t4AaABAg
  Have you ever played an instrument? If so, do you find it similar to learning a language?
    ↳ **@SovereignManChannel** (👍0 · 2026-08-08) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_6JxvW0FMQ8&lc=UgxJ548nbPxlBXgGM2t4AaABAg.AZqMMsAId4wA_Cdm2_j6_u
      I played classical guitar for 2 years + gave a concert at the age of 14. Now, I am teaching myself to play electric guitar (harder than doing it with a tutor, I know but ChatGPT is my savior on that).
      
      One of the biggest mistakes when learning to play an instrument is not a metronome. One of my tutors told me in our first lesson "Speed is a disaster. Focus on getting clean notes first." I practice with metronome since then.

**@netskillLab.a** (👍6 · 2026-08-05) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_6JxvW0FMQ8&lc=UgxvNnGjEaR3z-d09iB4AaABAg
  🇧🇮🇧🇮🇧🇮🇧🇮🇧🇮🇧🇮🇧🇮
  Good afternoon, Hadar. I hope you're doing well.
  
  I'd like to take this golden opportunity to congratulate you and thank you for sharing these kinds of videos. They have been incredibly helpful for non-native English speakers like me.

  Both are correct, but they are used in different regions:
  "Three hundred and fifty" is standard in British English (and other Commonwealth countries).
  "Three hundred fifty" is the primary style in American English, which drops the "and" between hundreds and tens.
  If you are aiming for American English, go with "three hundred fifty." (Also, note the spelling has two e's: three).
